Specific thanks to Chi Huynh for helping with paper formatting and running experiments, as well as Benno Stein for thoughtful discussions on goal-directed verification.
This research was supported in part by the National Science Foundation under grants
CCF-1619282, CCF-2008369, and
AID/CIEDS project FARO.

Data-Availability Statement

The full implementation and data used for this evaluation are available as an artifact on Zenodo
(
Meier et al. 2023
)
.
While the full experiments are resource-intensive computationally, a subset of the experiments may be run on an x86 Linux machine with 8GB of memory allocated to a Docker container.

Appendix A
Assumptions of the Application-Only Transition System

In practice, the translation from a compiled application to the application-only transition system makes some assumptions about the execution environment that we list in this section.
These assumptions simplify reasoning about concurrency, reflection, and library behavior.
First, we assume that all callbacks occur on the same thread (i.e., individual statements inside two separate callbacks cannot be interleaved during execution).
In practice, some callbacks do occur concurrently.
For example, the callback passed to
Single
.
create
(...)
in the place of the
...
is typically executed on a background thread.
Specific kinds of program analysis exist to address the interleaving caused by threaded concurrency, but that is out of scope for our paper.
Additionally, we do not consider exceptional control flow involving try/catch blocks.

For the application only transition system, we assume that the framework cannot directly modify the app store except through the invocation of a callback.
In theory, the framework may directly modify fields such as
this
.
act
generating an execution not represented by the application only transition system.
However, such direct access is rare.
Since the framework must be compiled without the application, any method or field that the framework accesses must typically extend a type declared by the framework (e.g.,
Fragment
which declares an abstract method
onCreate
).
The only exception is reflection, which may be used to access the app store such as the
this
.
act
field directly, but we assume such uses of reflection are rare, as it breaks encapsulation.
More commonly, the framework uses reflection to create instances of objects such as
PlayerFragment
.

Ali and Lhoták 2012
coined the phrase
separate-compilation assumption
to describe how a reasonable set of assumptions on framework behavior may be used to generate an application-only call graph. Our application-only transition system follows the same principle and is based on the same assumptions.

As mentioned in the Overview (Section
2
), we assume callin invocations do not
synchronously
call back to the app (i.e., a callin stays in framework code until it returns).
This assumption is frequently mirrored by other static analysis, such as
Arzt and Bodden 2016
, by framework stub implementations that do not invoke callbacks.
In general, we find that not many Android methods have synchronous callbacks.
In fact, none of our examples use a method exhibiting this behavior.

This assumption is reflected by the single
\mathit{\ell}\,\mathord{-}\mkern-4.0mu\mathord{[}\mathsf{ci}\,x^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{x})\mathord{]}\mkern-4.0mu\mathord{\shortrightarrow}\,\mathit{\ell}^{\prime}
boundary transition.
Our boundary stacks are degenerate here in that they will only ever have at most one activation
\mathit{k}
(for the active callback).
The boundary stack
\kappa
is conceptually the subsequence of the run-time call stack corresponding to boundary transitions (i.e., the pending calls alternating between callbacks and callins), which ensures that the message history consists of matching calls and returns.

One can extend the language to support synchronous callbacks by splitting the callin invocation boundary transition in two: one for the callin invocation into the framework (i.e., from
\mathit{\ell}
to
fwk
) and one for a call return back from the framework (i.e., from
fwk
to
\mathit{\ell}^{\prime}
), analogous to callback invocations and callback returns.
Note that if synchronous callbacks can be soundly modeled as asynchronous ones, then it is also unnecessary to add the complexity of synchronous callbacks (which is generally the case for Android).

Appendix B
Proofs for Message History Program Logic

In this section, we prove the theorems for the message history program logic (MHPL) that we describe in
Section 3
.
We make the following assumptions:

Assumption 1 (Application Step Soundness).

If
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash\mathit{t}
and
\langle\sigma^{\prime},\mathit{t}\rangle\Downarrow\sigma
and
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{post}(\mathit{t}))
then
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{pre}(\mathit{t}))
.

Providing a sound specification is the responsibility of the user of
Historia
.

Property 1 (All Prefixes are Realizable).

If
\omega;m\in\Omega
then
\omega\in\Omega
.

Property 2 (Sound Excludes Init).

If
\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\;\mathsf{excludesinit}
then
\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}\not\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
.

Property 3 (Entailment Soundness).

If
\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}^{\prime}
and
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}^{\prime}
then
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
.

The sound excludes init
Property
2
is a consequence of the sound encoding of message history program logics Section
5
, a sound first order logic encoding, and a sound SMT solver.

This case holds trivially.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\overline{v}=\overline{\rho(x)}\hskip 8.19447ptm=\mathsf{ci}\,v^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{v})\hskip 8.19447pt\omega;m\in\Omega}{\langle\mathit{\ell}\colon\omega\cdot\kappa\cdot\rho,\mathit{\ell}\,\mathord{-}\mkern-4.0mu\mathord{[}\mathsf{ci}\,x^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{x})\mathord{]}\mkern-4.0mu\mathord{\shortrightarrow}\,\mathit{\ell}^{\prime}\rangle\Downarrow^{\Omega}\mathit{\ell}^{\prime}\colon\omega;m\cdot\kappa\cdot\rho[x^{\prime}\mathbin{\mapsto}v^{\prime}]}\quad\textsc{c-callin-invoke}

The only judgment that applies is:

\frac{\widehat{\text{$\rho$}}=\widehat{\text{$\rho$}}^{\prime}\mathbin{\mbox{$\ast$}}\mathop{\mathop{\mathchoice{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{\mbox{$\ast$}}}{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{\mbox{$\ast$}}}{\mbox{$\ast$}}{\mbox{$\ast$}}}}\overline{x\mathbin{\mapstochar\shortrightarrow}\hat{x}}}{\vdash\{\mathit{\ell}\colon\mathsf{ci}\,\hat{x}^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{\hat{x}})\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}\cdot\widehat{\text{$\kappa$}}\cdot\widehat{\text{$\rho$}}\}\;\mathit{\ell}\,\mathord{-}\mkern-4.0mu\mathord{[}\mathsf{ci}\,x^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{x})\mathord{]}\mkern-4.0mu\mathord{\shortrightarrow}\,\mathit{\ell}^{\prime}\;\{\mathit{\ell}^{\prime}\colon\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}\cdot\widehat{\text{$\kappa$}}\cdot\widehat{\text{$\rho$}}\mathbin{\mbox{$\ast$}}x^{\prime}\mathbin{\mapstochar\shortrightarrow}\hat{x}^{\prime}\}}

a-callin-invoke

Let
\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}_{\text{post}}={\mathit{\ell}^{\prime}\colon\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}\cdot\widehat{\text{$\kappa$}}\cdot\widehat{\text{$\rho$}}\mathbin{\mbox{$\ast$}}x^{\prime}\mathbin{\mapstochar\shortrightarrow}\hat{x}^{\prime}}
.

Let
\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}_{\text{pre}}={\mathit{\ell}\colon\mathsf{ci}\,\hat{x}^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{\hat{x}})\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}\cdot\widehat{\text{$\kappa$}}\cdot\widehat{\text{$\rho$}}}
.

Let
\sigma_{\text{post}}={\mathit{\ell}^{\prime}\colon\omega;\mathsf{ci}\,v^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{v})\cdot\kappa\cdot\rho[x^{\prime}\mathbin{\mapsto}v^{\prime}]}

Let
\sigma_{\text{pre}}={\mathit{\ell}\colon\omega\cdot\kappa\cdot\rho}

We have that
\sigma_{\text{post}}\cdot\theta\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}_{\text{post}}
holds for any
\theta
such that
\theta=\theta^{\prime}\overline{[\hat{x}\mathbin{\mapsto}v]}[\hat{x}^{\prime}\mathbin{\mapsto}v^{\prime}]
by the definition of concretization.

We prove that
\sigma_{\text{pre}}\cdot\theta\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}_{\text{pre}}
.

We break this down into sub-cases for each part of the abstract state:

Sub Case: If
\omega;\mathsf{ci}\,v^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{v})\cdot\theta\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}
then
\omega\cdot\theta\models_{S}\mathsf{ci}\,\hat{x}^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{\hat{x}})\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}

\mathsf{ci}\,v^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{v})\cdot\theta\models_{S}\mathsf{ci}\,\hat{x}^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{\hat{x}})
since
\theta=\theta^{\prime}\overline{[\hat{x}\mathbin{\mapsto}v]}[\hat{x}^{\prime}\mathbin{\mapsto}v^{\prime}]

Since the premise of the lemma states
\Omega\subseteq\Omega_{S}
then for all
\omega
,
\omega\in\Omega
implies
\omega\models S
.

Then from
\omega;\mathsf{ci}\,v^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{v})\in\Omega
we have that
\omega;\mathsf{ci}\,v^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{v})\models S
.

Therefore,
\omega\cdot\theta\models_{S}\mathsf{ci}\,\hat{x}^{\prime}\,\mathit{md}(\overline{\hat{x}})\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}
holds by the definition of concretization.

Sub Case: If
\kappa\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$\kappa$}}
then
\kappa\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$\kappa$}}
holds trivially.

Sub Case: If
\rho[x^{\prime}\mathbin{\mapsto}v^{\prime}]\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$\rho$}}\mathbin{\mbox{$\ast$}}x^{\prime}\mathbin{\mapstochar\shortrightarrow}\hat{x}^{\prime}
then
\rho\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$\rho$}}
.

This case holds by the definition of concretization of app stores.

∎

Lemma 3.2
(
boundary-step soundness
) If
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{S}\mathit{b}
and
\langle\sigma^{\prime},\mathit{b}\rangle\Downarrow^{\Omega}\sigma
such that
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{post}(\mathit{b}))
and
\Omega\subseteq\Omega_{S}
, then
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{pre}(\mathit{b}))
.

Proof of
Lemma
3.2
.

By inversion on
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{S}\mathit{b}
we have
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{post}(\mathit{b}))\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
and
\vdash\{\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}^{\prime}\}\;\mathit{b}\;\{\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\}
and
\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}^{\prime}\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{pre}(\mathit{b}))
.

By applying
Property
3
, we have
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
.

By applying
Lemma
3.1
, we have
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}^{\prime}
.

By applying
Property
3
, we have
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{pre}(\mathit{b}))
.

∎

Lemma 3.3
(
inductive soundness
) If
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}

and
\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma

such that
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}

and
\Omega\subseteq\Omega_{S}
,
then
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{loc}(\sigma^{\prime}))
.

Proof of
Lemma
3.3
.

Proof by induction on the derivation
\mathcal{D}=~\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}}{\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma}\quad\textsc{c-transitive-step}

By inversion on
c-transitive-step
, we have
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
.

From
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
and by inversion on
A-Inductive
, we have
\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{loc}(\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}))
.  From
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
and
Property
3
we have
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{loc}(\sigma^{\prime}))
satisfying the consequent of Lemma 3.3.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow_{p}^{\Omega}\sigma}{\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma}\quad\textsc{c-trnasitive-step-inductive}

The inductive hypothesis states: If
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}^{\prime\prime}\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}^{\prime\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}^{\prime\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}
then
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}^{\prime\prime}(\operatorname{loc}(\sigma^{\prime}))
.

From the premise of Lemma 3.3, we assume
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
and
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma
.

By inversion on
c-transitive-step-inductive
we have
\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow_{p}^{\Omega}\sigma
.

By inversion on
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow_{p}^{\Omega}\sigma
we have two cases for an application and boundary step.

Sub Case
c-app-stepp
:
By inversion on
c-app-step
we have
\langle\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mathit{t}\rangle\Downarrow^{\Omega}\sigma
and
\mathit{t}\in\mathit{p}
.

By inversion from
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
we have

\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{loc}(\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}))
and

\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash\mathit{t}\;\;\text{for all $\mathit{t}\in\mathit{p}$}
.

Applying
Assumption
1
to
\langle\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mathit{t}\rangle\Downarrow\sigma
and
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash\mathit{t}
and
\mathit{t}\in\mathit{p}
we have
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{pre}(\mathit{t}))
and
\operatorname{pre}(\mathit{t})=\operatorname{loc}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{loc}(\sigma^{\prime}))
.

Sub Case
c-boundary-step
we have
\langle\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mathit{b}\rangle\Downarrow^{\Omega}\sigma
and
\mathit{b}\in\mathit{p}
.

By inversion from
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
we have

\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{loc}(\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}))
and

\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{S}\mathit{b}\;\;\text{for all $\mathit{b}\in\mathit{p}$}
.

Applying
Lemma
3.2
to
\langle\sigma^{\prime\prime},\mathit{b}\rangle\Downarrow^{\Omega}\sigma
and
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash\mathit{b}
and
\mathit{b}\in\mathit{p}
we have
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{pre}(\mathit{b}))
.

From the abstract boundary step rules
\operatorname{pre}(\mathit{b})=\operatorname{loc}(\sigma^{\prime\prime})
.

By the inductive hypothesis, we have
\sigma^{\prime}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\operatorname{loc}(\sigma^{\prime}))
.

∎

Theorem 3.4
(
refute soundness
) If
\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\;\mathsf{unreach}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma
and
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
and
\Omega\subseteq\Omega_{S}
then
\sigma^{\prime}\not=\hbox{
$\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}$
}
.

Proof of
Theorem 3.4
.

Proof by contradiction: if we assume that
\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\;\mathsf{unreach}
and
\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma
and
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
.

By inversion on
\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}\;\mathsf{unreach}
we know
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
and
\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\texttt{fwk})\;\mathsf{excludesinit}
then we can derive a contradiction.

From
\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}\vdash_{\mathit{p}}^{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
and
\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}\rightarrow^{\ast}\mathrel{\begin{subarray}{c}\Omega\\
p\end{subarray}}\sigma
and
Lemma
3.3
we have that
\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\texttt{fwk})
(note that
\operatorname{loc}(\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}})=\texttt{fwk}
).

By
Property
2
it must be the case that
\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}\not\models\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\texttt{fwk})
.

From
\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}\not\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\texttt{fwk})
and
\sigma_{\text{$\textrm{init}$}}\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\Sigma$}}(\texttt{fwk})
we derive a contradiction. Therefore, any
\sigma\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\sigma$}}
cannot be reached from the initial state.

∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Combining Abstract Message Histories with Callback Control-Flow

Proof of Theorem
5.1
(
1
).

In the forward direction (
1
) we proceed by induction on the derivation
\mathcal{D}=~\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{}{\vdash_{S}\mbox{{$\mathsf{okhist}$}}\equiv\text{true}}\quad\textsc{temporal-okhist}

If
\vdash_{S}\mbox{{$\mathsf{okhist}$}}\equiv\text{true}
and
\omega\cdot\theta\models_{S}\mbox{{$\mathsf{okhist}$}}
then
\omega\cdot\theta\models\text{true}
. This case holds trivially.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{S,\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}}{\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{temporal-hypmsg}\\

Let
\mathcal{D}_{1}
be the premise
\vdash_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
.

Assume
\omega\cdot\theta\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
.

Pick a concrete message
m_{1}
such that
m_{1}\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
as concrete message always exists for any
\widehat{\text{$m$}}
.

By the definition of concretization,
\models_{S}
, and since
\omega\cdot\theta\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}_{2}\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
if
m_{1}\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
then
\omega;m_{1}\cdot\theta\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}_{2}
.

By the inductive hypothesis on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with
\omega;m_{1}\cdot\theta\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}_{2}
, we have
\omega;m_{1}\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
.

From
\omega;m_{1}\cdot\theta\models_{S}\widehat{\text{$\omega$}}_{2}
, the base case of concretization, and the fact that realizable message histories are closed under prefix, we have that
\omega;m_{1}\models S
.

By Lemma
5.2
(
1
), and the premise
S,\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{D}
, and
\omega;m_{1}\models S
we have that
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
5.3
(
1
), and the premise
\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
of
\mathcal{D}
, and
\omega;m_{1}\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
we have that
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Therefore
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}
.

∎

(

Proof of Lemma
5.2
in the forward direction
1
.

Proof by induction over the derivation
\mathcal{D}=S,\widehat{\text{$m$}}\vdash\widetilde{\omega}
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\simeq_{\vartheta}\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}}{(\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}\boxright\widetilde{\omega}),\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash[\vartheta]\widetilde{\omega}}\quad\textsc{instantiate-yes}

From the lemma we have a history with a message appended models the specification
\omega;m_{1}\models(\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}\boxright\widetilde{\omega})
, there is a message in the abstract message
m_{1}\cdot\theta_{1}\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
.

From the premise
\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\simeq_{\vartheta}\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}
we have the capture avoiding substitution
\vartheta
such that
\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
is equal to
[\vartheta]\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}
.

From the semantics of CBCFTL in
Figure 7
, we have that
m_{2}\cdot\theta_{2}\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}
such that
\omega[i]=m_{2}
implies
\omega\cdot\theta_{2}\cdot i-1\models\widetilde{\omega}
.

Since the free variables of
\widetilde{\omega}
are a subset or equal to the free variables of
\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}
, the substitution,
[\vartheta]\widetilde{\omega}
, captures all free variables of
\widetilde{\omega}
. Since this substitution is capture avoiding, all the relevant bindings in
\theta_{2}
are swapped for the corresponding
\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
values and we can apply the implication from the CBCFTL semantics getting
\omega;m_{1}\cdot\theta_{1}\cdot\text{len}({\omega})-1\models[\vartheta]\widetilde{\omega}
which is equivalent to our theorem goal,
\omega\cdot\theta_{1}\cdot\text{len}({\omega})[\vartheta]\models\widetilde{\omega}
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\not\simeq\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}}{(\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}\boxright\widetilde{\omega}),\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash\text{true}}\quad\textsc{instantiate-no}\\

Trivially,
\omega\cdot\theta\cdot\text{len}({\omega})\models\text{true}
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{(S_{1}),\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\hskip 8.19447pt(S_{2}),\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt}{(S_{1}\wedge S_{2}),\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}}\quad\textsc{instantiate-and}

Since the semantics of intended for
\wedge
(elided from figure
7
for brevity) are that it is a conjunction of each instantiated specification, this case holds trivially.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{}{\text{true},\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash\text{true}}\quad\textsc{instantiate-true}

Since any history,
\omega
, is feasible under the “true” specification, this case holds trivially.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.2
in the backward direction
2
.

Proof by induction over the derivation
\mathcal{D}=S,\widehat{\text{$m$}}\vdash\widetilde{\omega}
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\simeq_{\vartheta}\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}}{(\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}\boxright\widetilde{\omega}),\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\vdash[\vartheta]\widetilde{\omega}}\quad\textsc{instantiate-yes}

From the lemma, we have that
\omega\cdot\theta_{1}\models(\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}\boxright\widetilde{\omega})
,
m_{1}\cdot\theta_{1}\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
, and
\omega\cdot\theta_{1}\models[\vartheta]\widetilde{\omega}
.

From the premise
\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}\simeq_{\vartheta}\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}
we have the capture avoiding substitution
\vartheta
such that
\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{1}
is equal to
[\vartheta]\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}
.

From the semantics of CBCFTL in
Figure 7
, we have that
m_{2}\cdot\theta_{2}\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}
such that
\omega[i]=m_{2}
implies
\omega\cdot\theta_{2}\cdot i-1\models\widetilde{\omega}
.

By similar reasoning as the proof for Lemma
5.2
(
1
) we know that any history and assignment such that
\omega\cdot\theta_{1}\models[\vartheta]\widetilde{\omega}
captures the variables shared between
[\vartheta]\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}
and

so any message such that

may be appended to a message history,
\omega
such that
\omega\models(\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}\boxright\widetilde{\omega})
and we know that
\omega;m\models(\widehat{\text{$m$}}_{2}\boxright\widetilde{\omega})
.

The other cases are similar to the forward proof.

∎

For the following proofs, we give the definition of
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
as the following:

(1)

If the names and message types of
\widetilde{m}
and
\widehat{\text{$m$}}
are the same, then
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
results in a conjunction of comparisons between each free variable of
\widetilde{m}
and the corresponding
\widehat{\text{$m$}}
variable.

(2)

If the names or message types of
\widetilde{m}
and
\widehat{\text{$m$}}
are different, then
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
is false.

We define
\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
as the negation of
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
in the forward direction
1
.

Proof by induction on the derivation
\mathcal{D}=\vdash\widetilde{\omega}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}
. We reference the CBCFTL semantics in
Figure 7
and the judgments in
Figure 8
.

In the following, let
\operatorname{fv}(m)=\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m})
be the conjunction comparing each variable not quantified in the expansion of
\widetilde{m}
into its definition
\exists\overline{\hat{x}}.\widehat{\text{$m$}}^{\prime}
to the corresponding value of
m
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{}{\vdash\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m}\equiv\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m})\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}\quad\textsc{quotient-once}

From the lemma we have that,
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m}
and
m\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}
. There are two subcases to consider:

Subcase 1: the message names and types are equal,
m\simeq_{\vartheta}\widetilde{m}
.

From the semantics of Once,
\operatorname{fv}(m){\widetilde{m}}
implies
\omega\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m}
. By the definition of message equivalence (
1
), this is equivalent to
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m})

Subcase 2: the names or message types are not equal,
m\not\simeq\widetilde{m}
are not equal.

From the semantics of Once,
\omega\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m}
must hold since
m
cannot match
\widetilde{m}
.

Under the assumption that the name or message type does not match
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m})\equiv\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m}
by the definition of message equivalence (
2
).

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{}{\vdash\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}\equiv\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}\quad\textsc{quotient-historically-not}

From the lemma we have that,
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}
and
m\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}
.

From the semantics of Has Not,
\omega\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}
. This holds for the formula
\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{}{\vdash\widetilde{m}_{2}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{1}\equiv\text{Match(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\widetilde{m}_{2}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{1}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{2},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)})\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}\quad\textsc{quotient-not-since}

From the lemma we have that,
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{m}_{1}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{2}
and
m\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}
. There are three subcases to consider:

Subcase 1: the message names and types are equal between the positive message,
m\simeq_{\vartheta}\widetilde{m}_{2}
.

From the semantics of Not Since: not
\operatorname{fv}(m)=\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m}_{2})
implies
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{m}_{1}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{2}
. This is implied by the formula
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\widetilde{m}_{2}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{1}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{2},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)})
since the first disjunction is false due to the definition of message equivalence.

Subcase 2: the message names and types are equal between the negative message,
m\simeq_{\vartheta}\widetilde{m}_{1}
.

From the semantics of Not Since: not
\operatorname{fv}(m)=\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m}_{1})
implies
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{m}_{1}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{2}
. This is implied by the formula
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\widetilde{m}_{2}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{1}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{2},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)})
since the first disjunction is false due to the definition of message equivalence.

Subcase 3: the message names and types are not equal to either message,
m\not\simeq\widetilde{m}_{1}
and
m\not\simeq\widetilde{m}_{2}
.

From the semantics of Not Since:
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{m}_{1}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{2}
. This is implied by the formula
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\widetilde{m}_{2}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{1}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{2},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)})
since
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
and
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m}_{2},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
are both false by the definition of message equivalence.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}\hskip 8.19447pt\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}{\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}\quad\textsc{quotient-and}

From the lemma and the judgment, quotient-and, we have
m\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}
,
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
,
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{1}
implies
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}
, and
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
implies
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}
.

By the semantics of and (elided from
Figure 7
),
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
, and the two implications, we have that
\omega\cdot\theta\models{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}}
.

The cases for
\vee
,
\forall\hat{x}.\widetilde{\omega}
, and
\exists\hat{x}.\widetilde{\omega}
are similar to
\wedge
.

For all the following productions, the message history is not affected:
\hat{x}_{1}=\hat{x}_{2}
,
\hat{x}_{1}\neq\hat{x}_{2}
, and “
true
”. Therefore, they model any history
\omega\cdot\theta
.

For the case “false”, it cannot model
\omega\cdot\theta
therefore holds vacuously.

∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
in the backward direction
2
.

Proof by induction on the derivation
\mathcal{D}=\vdash\widetilde{\omega}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}
. We reference the CBCFTL semantics in
Figure 7
and the judgments in
Figure 8
.

Let
\operatorname{fv}(m)=\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m})
be the conjunction of comparing each variable not quantified in the expansion of
\widetilde{m}
into its definition
\exists\overline{\hat{x}}.\widehat{\text{$m$}}^{\prime}
to the corresponding value of
m
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{}{\vdash\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m}\equiv\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m})\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}\quad\textsc{quotient-once}

From the lemma we have that
\omega\cdot\theta\models\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m})
and
m\cdot\theta\models{\widehat{\text{$m$}}}
. We consider two different subcases, in the case the message names are the same or not.

Assume the message names matches,
m\simeq_{\vartheta}\widetilde{m}
. It is either the case that the arguments match,
\operatorname{fv}(m)=\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m})
, and
\theta(\widetilde{m})=m
making
i
for Once equals
\text{len}({\omega;m})
or the arguments don’t match, not
\operatorname{fv}(m)=\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m})
, and the
i
for once is in
\omega
. In both cases
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m}
.

Assume the message names do not match,
m\not\simeq\widetilde{m}
. In this case
\text{Match(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
is false so the
i
for once must be in
\omega
, therefore
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{O}~\widetilde{m}
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{}{\vdash\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}\equiv\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}\quad\textsc{quotient-historically-not}

From the lemma we have that
\omega\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
and
m\cdot\theta\models{\widehat{\text{$m$}}}
.

From the definition of message not equals, we know either
m\not\simeq\widetilde{m}
or not
\operatorname{fv}(m)=\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m})
so
\theta(\widetilde{m})\neq m
.

From the definition of Historically Not we have that
\omega\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}
.

Since we have
\theta(\widetilde{m})\neq m
and
\omega\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}
, by the definition of Historically Not, we have that
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\mathbf{HN}~\widetilde{m}
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{}{\vdash\widetilde{m}_{2}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{1}\equiv\text{Match(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\widetilde{m}_{2}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{1}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{2},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)})\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}\quad\textsc{quotient-not-since}

From the lemma we have that
\omega\cdot\theta\models\text{Match(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\vee(\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}\wedge\widetilde{m}_{2}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{1}\wedge\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{2},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)})
and
m\cdot\theta\models{\widehat{\text{$m$}}}
. We have two subcases:

Subcase
m\simeq_{\vartheta}\widetilde{m}_{2}
and
\operatorname{fv}(m)=\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m}_{2})
: In this case the
i
from the definition of Not Since is at the last message,
m
, and
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{m}_{1}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{2}
.

Subcase
m\not\simeq\widetilde{m}_{2}
or
\operatorname{fv}(m)\neq\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m}_{2})
: In this case, we have
\text{NotMatch(}\widetilde{m}_{1},\widehat{\text{$m$}}\text{)}
so the
i
in Not Since must be in
\omega
.

by the definition of message not equals, we know that
\widetilde{m}_{1}\not\simeq\widehat{\text{$m$}}
or
\operatorname{fv}(\widehat{\text{$m$}})\neq\operatorname{fv}(\widetilde{m}_{1})
so
m
must not be equal to
\widetilde{m}
under assignment
\theta
and therefore
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{m}_{1}~\mathbf{NS}~\widetilde{m}_{2}
.

Case
\mathcal{D}=\frac{\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}\hskip 8.19447pt\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}{\vdash\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}\equiv\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}\fatsemi\widehat{\text{$m$}}}\quad\textsc{quotient-and}

From the lemma and the quotient-and judgment, we have that
m\cdot\theta\models\widehat{\text{$m$}}
,
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}
,
\omega\cdot\theta\models{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}^{\prime}}
implies
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}}
, and
\omega\cdot\theta\models{\widetilde{\omega}_{2}^{\prime}}
implies
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models{\widetilde{\omega}_{2}}
.

By the straightforward semantics of and (elided from
Figure 7
),
\omega\cdot\theta\models\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}
, and the two implications, we have that
\omega;m\cdot\theta\models{\widetilde{\omega}_{1}\wedge\widetilde{\omega}_{2}}
.

The cases for
\vee
,
\forall\hat{x}.\widetilde{\omega}
, and
\exists\hat{x}.\widetilde{\omega}
are similar to
\wedge
.

For all the following productions, the message history is not affected:
\hat{x}_{1}=\hat{x}_{2}
,
\hat{x}_{1}\neq\hat{x}_{2}
, and “
true
”. Therefore, they model any history
\omega;\cdot\theta
.

For the case “false”, it cannot model
\omega;m\cdot\theta
therefore holds vacuously.

∎

Appendix D
Extended Explanations of the Benchmark Applications and Specifications

Here, we give a listing of all the specifications we wrote for Section
6
RQ1,
details on the crashes, and reasons the Flowdroid model would not be able to classify the benchmarks correctly.

The first benchmark,
{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{getAct}}}}}}^{[\ref{spec:getActivityNull}]}

, is a slightly more complex version of the motivating example in Section
2
.
The main difference is that instead of an
act
field, the app invokes the callin
getActivity
.
Calling
getActivity
before
onCreate
or after
onDestroy
results in a null value as captured by History Implication
3
.
As a minor difference,
{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{getAct}}}}}}^{[\ref{spec:getActivityNull}]}

uses the
onActivityCreated
callback instead of
onDestroy
which has slightly more complicated behavior (History Implication
4
).
The behavior of the
call
callback was explained in Section
2
with History Implication
1
.

History Implication 2.

For all
f
, if the framework invokes
f
.
onCreate
()
, the same message
{\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onCreate}}()}}}}
is
H
istorially
N
ot possible (or,
H
as
N
ever been invoked in the past).

{{\displaystyle\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onCreate}}()}}}}\boxright\mathbf{HN}~\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onCreate}}()}}}}

History Implication 3.

The method
getActivity
returns null when invoked on an
Activity
in the paused state.

{{{{{{{\displaystyle\mathsf{ci}~\hbox{$\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{null}}}}}}}$}=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}}}}}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{getActivity}}(}}}}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language)}}}}\boxright\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onActivityCreated}}()}}}}~\mathbf{NS}~\mathsf{cb}\mathsf{ret}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onDestroy}}()}}}}\vee\mathbf{HN}~\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onActivityCreated}}()}}}}

History Implication 4.

For a given
Fragment
instance, either
onActivityCreated
has not been invoked or
onDestroy
has not been invoked since
onActivityCreated
.

{\displaystyle\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onActivityCreated}}()}}}}\boxright

{\displaystyle\mathbf{HN}~\mathsf{cb}\mathsf{ret}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onDestroy}}()}}}}\wedge

{{\displaystyle\mathbf{HN}~\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onActivityCreated}}()}}}}\wedge\mathbf{HN}~\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onActivityCreated}}()}}}}

Flowdroid would not alarm on the buggy version of
{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{getAct}}}}}}^{[\ref{spec:getActivityNull}]}

because
call
was not in the call graph making the assertion unreachable.
If
call
was added to the framework model generation (e.g. by
Cao et al. 2015
), then the Flowdroid model would not be able to capture the effect of
unsubscribe
(i.e. History Implication
1
) resulting in a false positive for the fix.

The second benchmark,
{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{execute}}}}}}^{[\ref{spec:execute}]}

, has a button that starts an
AsyncTask
to perform an action in the background using the
execute
callin.

AsyncTask
is an abstract class that can be overridden by the app to encapsulate long running tasks (similar to
Single
).
In order to prevent concurrency issues in the state of the overridden class, the framework enforces that
execute
crashes if called twice on the same instance of the overridden class.
We capture this exceptional return with History Implication
5
.
If the button was clicked twice quickly, the task could be executed twice crashing the application.
The fix was to disable the button using
b
.
setEnabled
(
false
)
(History Implication
6
).
Additionally, if the listener could be registered to two different buttons via two calls to
onCreate
which calls
setOnClickListener
, then the second button could be pressed crashing the app.
To rule out this case, we needed History Implication
2
(also needed by
{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{getAct}}}}}}^{[\ref{spec:getActivityNull}]}

).

History Implication 5.

For any given instance of
AsyncTask
, the
execute
method throws an exception if
execute
has been invoked in the past.

{{{\displaystyle\hbox{$\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{exn}}}}}}}$}~\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{execute}}()}}}}\boxright\mathbf{O}~\mathsf{ci}\mathsf{ret}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{t}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{execute}}()}}}}

History Implication 6.

Every time the
onClick
callback occur, the associated button has not been disabled.

{{\displaystyle\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{l}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onClick}}()}}}}\boxright\exists\ \text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}}}}}.\

{\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{O}~\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{setOnClickListener}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{l}}})}}}}\land\right.

{{{{{{{{{\displaystyle\left.(\mathbf{HN}~\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{setEnabled}}(}}}}\hbox{$\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{false}}}}}}}$}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language)}}}}\lor\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{setEnabled}}(}}}}\hbox{$\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{false}}}}}}}$}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language)}}}}~\mathbf{NS}~\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{v}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{setEnabled}}(}}}}\hbox{$\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{true}}}}}}}$}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language)}}}})\right)

The fix for
{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{execute}}}}}}^{[\ref{spec:execute}]}

would be misclassified by Flowdroid’s model because the effect of the callin
b
.
setEnable
(
false
)
on the button is not captured.

The third benchmark,
{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{dismiss}}}}}}^{[\ref{spec:dismiss}]}

, uses a background task (via
AsyncTask
) and when the task finishes, the
onPostExecute
callback dismisses a
ProgressDialog
. If the
ProgressDialog
is dismissed after the parent has been paused, it throws an exception (History implication
7
).
The fix was to check if the parent UI was visible before dismissing the dialog using the application state.
Additionally, we needed to restrict the method used to create the dialog,
show
, to return a fresh dialog instance each time (History Implication
8
).

History Implication 7.

The
dismiss
callin throws an exception if invoked while the attached
Activity
is paused.

{{{{{{{\displaystyle\hbox{$\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{exn}}}}}}}$}~\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{d}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{dismiss}}()}}}}\boxright\exists\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}}}}}.~\mathbf{O}~\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{d}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}:={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{\textunderscore}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{show}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}})}}}}\wedge\mathbf{HN}~\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onResume}}()}}}}\vee\mathsf{cb}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onResume}}()}}}}~\mathbf{NS}~\mathsf{cb}\mathsf{ret}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{a}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{onPause}}()}}}}

History Implication 8.

When the
show
callin returns an object, it must have been the case that the object was not returned by a different
show
in the past.

{{{{{{{{\displaystyle\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{d}}}}}}}=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{b}}}}}}}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{show}}(}}}}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language)}}}}\boxright\mathbf{HN}~\mathsf{ci}~\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{d}}}}}}}=\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{\textunderscore}}}}}}}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{show}}(}}}}\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language)}}}}

The bug for
{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{dismiss}}}}}}^{[\ref{spec:dismiss}]}

would not be detected by Flowdroid because the
onPostExecute
callback was not in the call graph.
Adding
onPostExecute
to the Flowdroid would result in a false alarm on the fix because the interaction between
show
,
dismiss
,
onPause
, and
onResume
captured by History Implication
7
could not be captured due to the callins.

The fourth benchmark,
{{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph2}{finish}}}}}}^{\hbox{$\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{null}}}}}}}$}}

, has a button that dereferences a field in a
onClick
callback. This field is set to
null
when the
Activity
is paused (via
onPause
) to save memory. However, via what may be considered a bug in some versions of the Android framework itself, calling
finish
on an
Activity
can result in the
onClick
callback occurring after
onPause
(History Implication
9
).
Similar to other benchmarks, there is a
onCreate
that registers the button and can only happen once (History Implication
2
).
Since an
Activity
may have multiple simultaneous instances in an Android app, we also need a spec that says a button may only come from one
Activity
instance (History Implication
10
).

History Implication 9.

Every time the
onClick
callback happens on the listener object
l
, the listener was registered on a
Button
object
v
, and the
Activity
object
a
was either in the “resumed state” (i.e., after a
onResume
but before than a
onPause
) or the message
finish
happened.

on an

The fifth benchmark

{{\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{subs}}}}}}^{\hbox{$\text{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@language{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{null}}}}}}}$}}

is caused by a dereference of a nullable field callback that is used by RXJava for synchronizing the results of a background task with the UI thread.
Similar to the first benchmark, this bug would not be detected by a whole-program analysis using the Flowdroid main method because the synchronization callback is missing from the call graph.

The

callin prevents calls to the subscribe callback.
